# TC-2026-10-17-9: an explicit cubic finite-order unitary with optimal classical mixing and vanishing cooperon

**Status.** This is a new fully explicit, number-theoretically controlled sequence **on an infinite prime subsequence**, satisfying all three quantitative input inequalities with fixed constants, while retaining a maximally non-generic deterministic spectral recurrence at the Heisenberg time. It is a rigorous stress test and an obstruction to strengthening the given hypotheses into deterministic long-time quantum mixing. It is **not** a counterexample to the random-phase two-channel transmission law: no nonuniform limit for its phase-averaged transmission has been established.

## CP1. The cubic circulant construction

Let `p>3` be prime with `p\equiv2\pmod 3`; there are infinitely many such primes (primes `5 mod 6`). Set `N=p`, `\omega=\exp(2\pi i/p)`, and define the real-valued sequence

$$
 h_p(d)=\frac1p\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\omega^{k^3+kd},\qquad d\in\mathbb F_p,
\tag{CP1}
$$

and the circulant matrix

$$
\boxed{\quad S_p(x,y)=h_p(x-y),\qquad x,y\in\mathbb F_p.\quad}
\tag{CP2}
$$

The sequence `h_p` is real because changing `k` to `-k` conjugates every summand in (CP1). Since cubing is a bijection of `\mathbb F_p` when `\gcd(3,p-1)=1`, one also has

$$
 h_p(0)=\frac1p\sum_{k}\omega^{k^3}=0.
\tag{CP3}
$$

Let `\mathcal F_p` be the discrete Fourier matrix. Fourier inversion gives

$$
 \boxed{\quad S_p=\mathcal F_p^*\operatorname{diag}(\omega^{k^3})\mathcal F_p
 \quad}\tag{CP4}
$$

with the Fourier convention adjusted to `(CP1)`. Hence `S_p` is a **real orthogonal** matrix, has zero diagonal, and has exactly the `p` distinct `p`-th roots of unity as eigenvalues. In particular,

$$
\boxed{S_p^p=I_p,\qquad \operatorname{Tr}S_p^p=p,\qquad
\operatorname{Tr}S_p^r=0\quad(1\le r<p).}
\tag{CP5}
$$

The Weil bound for additive character sums of degree three gives

$$
|h_p(d)|\le\frac2{\sqrt p}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\boxed{\max_{x,y}|S_p(x,y)|^2\le\frac4p.}
\tag{CP6}
$$

This classical bound is valid because `p>3` and the cubic coefficient is nonzero modulo `p`. See A. Weil's polynomial additive-character bound, e.g. *Bounded Gaps Between Primes*, Cambridge University Press, Appendix E, DOI `10.1017/9781108872201.016`, or the open-access theorem at `https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10941333/`.

## CP2. The classical spectral gap is exactly `p^{-1/2}`

Write `P_{xy}=|h_p(x-y)|^2`. Its Fourier multiplier at frequency `m\in\mathbb F_p` is, by Fourier orthogonality,

$$
\widehat P(m)=\sum_d|h_p(d)|^2\omega^{-md}
=\frac1p\sum_k\omega^{k^3-(k-m)^3}.
\tag{CP7}
$$

At `m=0` this multiplier is `1`. At every `m\ne0`, the exponent polynomial equals

$$
k^3-(k-m)^3=3mk^2-3m^2k+m^3,
$$

which is a nondegenerate quadratic polynomial modulo `p`. Its Gauss sum has absolute value `\sqrt p`. Therefore

$$
\boxed{\quad
\|P_p-J_p\|_{\rm op}=p^{-1/2}.
\quad}
\tag{CP8}
$$

This is an **exact**, not asymptotic, spectral-gap evaluation. In particular, the requested numerical threshold `1/100` holds for every such `p\ge10007`.

## CP3. The cooperon norm is at most `2p^{-1/2}`

Because `S_p` is real, its cooperon is the symmetric real circulant matrix

$$
 B_p(x,y)=h_p(x-y)h_p(y-x).
$$

Its Fourier multiplier is again computed by orthogonality:

$$
 \widehat B_p(m)
 =\frac1p\sum_{k\in\mathbb F_p}\omega^{k^3+(k-m)^3}.
\tag{CP9}
$$

Since `p>3`, this is a degree-three additive-character sum with leading coefficient `2` modulo `p`. Weil's estimate applies at **every** frequency, giving

$$
\boxed{\quad\|B_p\|_{\rm op}\le\frac2{\sqrt p}.\quad}
\tag{CP10}
$$

In fact, `\widehat B_p(0)=0`, since `k\mapsto k^3` is a permutation and `2\ne0\pmod p`.

Thus on the infinite subsequence of primes `p\equiv2\pmod3` the cubic family satisfies **all three hypotheses**, with fixed constants, e.g.

$$
\boxed{
 C_0=4,\quad \rho_*=\frac12,\quad
 \max |S_{xy}|^2\le\frac4p,\quad
 \|P-J\|=\frac1{\sqrt p}\le\frac1{100},\quad
 \|B\|\le\frac2{\sqrt p}\le\frac12
}
\tag{CP11}
$$

for sufficiently large eligible `p`.

Yet `S_p^p=I_p` exactly: no hypothesis controls deterministic quantum recurrences at the Heisenberg scale. Hence a proposed proof cannot silently replace classical bistochastic mixing plus cooperon suppression by a uniform-in-time bound on `\|S_N^t\|` or by the absence of exact quantum recurrences. (Unitarity already gives `\|S_N^t\|=1`, but the stronger statement concerns the nontrivial trace `\operatorname{Tr}S_N^p=p`.) The independent diagonal phases in the **actual** ensemble `D_pS_p` destroy this simple `p`-step identity, so this observation alone has **no bearing on the truth** of `T_N\Rightarrow U[0,1]`.

**Numerical verification.** The short FFT program `tc_cubic_circulant.py`, reproduced below, checks the exact `\|P-J\|\sqrt p=1`, the Weil bounds `\|B\|\sqrt p\le2` and `p\max|S_{xy}|^2\le4`, and the real-valuedness of `h_p`, for `p=5,11,17,23,29,41,101,503,1019,5003,10007`. For the final `p=10007`, it gives flatness constant `3.97705`, classical gap `1/\sqrt{10007}<.01`, and cooperon multiplier bound `1.99208/\sqrt{10007}<.02`. This is a computational check of the already rigorous finite-field argument, **not a Monte Carlo test of the transmission conjecture**.

### Complete verification code

```python
import numpy as np
for p in (5,11,17,23,29,41,101,503,1019,5003,10007):
 if p%3 != 2:
  continue
 k=np.arange(p,dtype=np.int64)
 omega=np.exp(2j*np.pi*((k*k*k)%p)/p)
 h=np.fft.ifft(omega)
 P=np.abs(h)**2
 G=(h*np.conj(h[(-k)%p]))
 eigP=np.fft.fft(P)
 eigB=np.fft.fft(G)
 opP=np.max(np.abs(eigP[1:]))
 opB=np.max(np.abs(eigB))
 print('p=',p,'flatC0=',round(p*np.max(np.abs(h)**2),5),'Pgap*sqrtp=',round(opP*np.sqrt(p),8),'Bnorm*sqrtp=',round(opB*np.sqrt(p),8),'imag_h=',np.max(abs(h.imag)),flush=True)
```


### Explicit phase-ensemble test of the cubic family (diagnostic only)

The actual independent-internal-phase Schur transmission was sampled with **uniformly random ordered external ports** and fresh internal phases, rather than studying the bare deterministic map. Direct dense linear solves (seed `20261017`, single-threaded BLAS) gave:

| prime `p` | trials | `mean(T)` | estimated MC SE | `mean(T^2)` | max grid CDF distance from `t` |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 11 | 6000 | 0.54848 | 0.00376 | 0.38579 | 0.077 |
| 23 | 5000 | 0.52652 | 0.00409 | 0.36099 | 0.042 |
| 41 | 4000 | 0.51720 | 0.00456 | 0.35082 | 0.029 |
| 101 | 1700 | 0.50541 | 0.00697 | 0.33807 | 0.020 |
| 503 | 100 | 0.47304 | 0.03049 | 0.31581 | 0.065 |

The uniform benchmark is `E[T]=1/2`, `E[T^2]=1/3`. At the sizes for which many independent draws were feasible, the data move **toward**, not away from, these targets. In particular, the `p=503` row is much too noisy to support a claim of an anomaly. This is neither a proof of universality nor an asymptotic counterexample. The rigorous content of this appendix is (CP1)--(CP11), and the simulation merely rejects a speculative counterexample mechanism at tested sizes.

```python
import os
os.environ.setdefault('OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS','1')
import numpy as np
from scipy.linalg import solve
rng=np.random.default_rng(20261017)
for p, nsim in [(11,6000),(23,5000),(41,4000),(101,1700),(503,100)]:
 if p%3!=2: continue
 k=np.arange(p,dtype=np.int64)
 h=np.fft.ifft(np.exp(2j*np.pi*(k*k*k%p)/p))
 S=h[(k[:,None]-k[None,:])%p]
 out=np.zeros(nsim)
 for t in range(nsim):
  aa=rng.choice(p,2,replace=False)
  a,b=int(aa[0]),int(aa[1]); E=[x for x in range(p) if x!=a and x!=b]
  z=np.exp(2j*np.pi*rng.random(p-2))
  KK=np.eye(p-2)-z[:,None]*S[np.ix_(E,E)]
  amp=S[a,b]+S[a,E]@solve(KK,z*S[E,b],assume_a='gen',check_finite=False)
  out[t]=abs(amp)**2
 print('p',p,'n',nsim,'ET',float(out.mean()),'SE',float(out.std(ddof=1)/nsim**.5),'ET2',float(np.mean(out*out)),'KSgrid',round(max(abs(np.mean(out<=x)-x) for x in np.linspace(0,1,41)),3),flush=True)
```

# TC-2026-10-17-10: deterministic classical equipartition, the first cooperon contraction, and a rigorously localized quantum time window

**Exact status.** This appendix proves three new results directly from the original hypotheses (some require fewer assumptions). It does not prove `T_N => Uniform[0,1]` and does not produce an admissible counterexample. The classical approximation below must **not** be substituted for the quantum phase-averaged Schur complement: their difference already need not vanish at fixed dimension. The result isolates the genuinely coherent obstruction.

## CE1. Classical absorption Schur complements are uniformly equidistributed

Let `P` be an `N x N` **doubly stochastic** matrix satisfying

$$
\max_{i,j}P_{ij}\le C/N,\qquad \|P-J_N\|_{\rm op}\le\varepsilon<1,
\tag{CE1}
$$

where `C>=1`, `epsilon<1` are constants. Choose **any** retained coordinate set `A` with `m=|A|`, and put `E=A^c`, `n=N-m`. Define the classical absorption matrix

$$
K_A^{\rm cl}=P_{AA}+P_{AE}(I-P_{EE})^{-1}P_{EA}.
\tag{CE2}
$$

The inverse exists: writing `R=P-J`, we have `||R||<=epsilon`, and if a vector supported on `E` were a unit-modulus eigenvector of `P_EE`, extension by zero to the full space would contradict the strict contraction `||P|_{1^perp}||<=epsilon`. More directly, `||P_EE||<1` because, for any nonzero vector `x` supported in the proper subset `E`, orthogonality of `Jx` and `Rx` gives `||Px||^2 <= ||Jx||^2+epsilon^2||x-Jx||^2 <||x||^2`.

Set

$$
\beta=C+1,\qquad
\gamma=1+\frac{\beta}{1-\varepsilon},\qquad
\kappa=C+\frac{\sqrt C\,\beta}{1-\varepsilon}.
\tag{CE3}
$$

**Theorem CE (nonasymptotic, uniform in the chosen set).** Whenever

$$
1\le m\le\frac{N}{2\beta\gamma},
\tag{CE4}
$$

we have the explicit estimate

$$
\boxed{\quad
\max_{a,b\in A}\left|(K_A^{\rm cl})_{ab}-\frac1m\right|
\le \frac1N\left[
C+\frac{C}{1-\varepsilon}+4\kappa+2\beta\gamma
+\frac{\kappa^2}{\beta\gamma}
\right].\quad}
\tag{CE5}
$$

Consequently, with the constant in square brackets denoted by `K(C,epsilon)`,

$$
\boxed{\quad
\|K_A^{\rm cl}-J_m\|_{\rm op}
\le K(C,\varepsilon)\frac{m}{N}.
\quad}
\tag{CE6}
$$

This holds *deterministically*, for **every** selected set, and for growing `m=o(N)` as well as fixed `m`. The matrix `K_A^{cl}` is itself nonnegative and doubly stochastic. In particular for `m=2` it has exactly the form

$$
K_A^{\rm cl}=\begin{pmatrix}1-t_A&t_A\\t_A&1-t_A\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\boxed{|t_A-1/2|\le K(C,\varepsilon)/N.}
\tag{CE7}
$$

### Proof of CE (all constants exposed)

Abbreviate

$$
A_0=P_{AA},\quad W=P_{AE},\quad V=P_{EA},\quad
R=P-J_N,\quad G=(I_E-R_{EE})^{-1},\quad e=\mathbf1_E.
\tag{CE8}
$$

Since `||R_EE||<=epsilon`, `||G||<=1/(1-epsilon)`. The rank-one Sherman--Morrison identity gives the **exact** representation

$$
K_A^{\rm cl}=A_0+WGV+
\frac{(WGe)(e^TGV)}{d},\qquad
 d=N-e^TGe.
\tag{CE9}
$$

For sufficient `N` as in (CE4), the estimates below show `d>=m/2`; thus the denominator is indeed nonzero. Every entry of `R` is bounded by `beta/N`. The zero row and column sums of `R` yield

$$
R_{EE}e=-R_{EA}\mathbf1_A,\qquad
e^TR_{EE}=-\mathbf1_A^TR_{AE},\qquad
e^TR_{EE}e=\mathbf1_A^TR_{AA}\mathbf1_A.
\tag{CE10}
$$

It follows that

$$
\|Ge-e\|_2,\ \|e^TG-e^T\|_2
\le\frac{\beta m}{(1-\varepsilon)\sqrt N}.
\tag{CE11}
$$

Also, since `G-I=R_EE G`,

$$
\begin{aligned}
|e^TGe-(N-m)|
&=|e^TR_{EE}Ge|\\
&\le |e^TR_{EE}e|+\|e^TR_{EE}\|_2\|Ge-e\|_2\\
&\le\frac{\beta m^2}{N}
\left(1+\frac{\beta}{1-\varepsilon}\right)
=\frac{\beta\gamma m^2}{N}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{CE12}
$$

Thus `|d-m|<=beta gamma m²/N`; (CE4) implies `d>=m/2`.

Every row of `W` and every column of `V` has Euclidean norm at most `sqrt(C/N)`: nonnegative entries are `<=C/N` and their sums are at most one. Since `We=1_A-A_0 1_A` and `e^TV=1_A^T-1_A^T A_0`, this implies, **entry by entry**,

$$
\boxed{
|(WGe)_a-1|\le\frac{\kappa m}{N},\qquad
|(e^TGV)_b-1|\le\frac{\kappa m}{N}.
}
\tag{CE13}
$$

Furthermore,

$$
|(WGV)_{ab}|\le\frac{C}{(1-\varepsilon)N},
\qquad (A_0)_{ab}\le C/N.
\tag{CE14}
$$

Write `x_a=(WGe)_a`, `y_b=(e^TGV)_b`. The preceding estimates give

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left|\frac{x_ay_b}{d}-\frac1m\right|
&\le\frac{|(x_a-1)+(y_b-1)+(x_a-1)(y_b-1)|}{d}
+\left|\frac1d-\frac1m\right|\\
&\le\frac{4\kappa}{N}+\frac{2\kappa^2m}{N^2}
+\frac{2\beta\gamma}{N}\\
&\le\frac1N\left(4\kappa+2\beta\gamma+\frac{\kappa^2}{\beta\gamma}\right),
\end{aligned}
\tag{CE15}
$$

where the final bound uses (CE4). Combining this with (CE9) and (CE14) proves (CE5). The operator-norm bound (CE6) follows from `||M||op<=m max_ij |M_ij|`. Nonnegativity of (CE2) is immediate. To check stochasticity, use `(I-P_EE)1_E=P_EA 1_A` and its transpose, together with the full row and column sums of `P`; then `K_A^{cl} 1_A=1_A` and `1_A^TK_A^{cl}=1_A^T`. A two-by-two doubly stochastic matrix has form (CE7). QED.

### Classical mean exit time has the same deterministic first-order scale

Starting in `a in A`, run the Markov chain with transition matrix `P`, and stop at the first return to `A`. Let `L_cl` be the number of internal visits in `E` prior to the return (zero for an immediate `A -> A` transition). Then

$$
\mathbb E_a L_{\rm cl} = [W(I-P_{EE})^{-1}e]_a
=\frac{N}{d}(WGe)_a,
\tag{CE16}
$$

by (CE9) and Sherman--Morrison. Therefore, under (CE4),

$$
\boxed{\qquad
\left|\frac{m}{N}\mathbb E_aL_{\rm cl}-1\right|
\le2(\kappa+\beta\gamma)\frac{m}{N},
\qquad a\in A.
\qquad}
\tag{CE17}
$$

In particular for fixed `m`, `E_a L_cl=N/m+O_{C,epsilon,m}(1)`, uniformly in `a,A`. This confirms *classical* escape on the Heisenberg scale, without claiming that the quantum process agrees with the Markov chain.

## QC1. The exact matched-occupation-path decomposition

Return to the original unitary `S`. Fix two distinct external ports `a,b` and `E=[N]\setminus\{a,b\}`. For each internal path `\gamma=(e_1,...,e_L) in E^L` define

$$
A_\gamma=S_{ae_1}S_{e_1e_2}\cdots S_{e_{L-1}e_L}S_{e_Lb},
\qquad z_\gamma=\prod_{r=1}^L z_{e_r}.
\tag{QC1}
$$

The multivariate Taylor coefficients of the two-port amplitude are

$$
F_{ab}(z)=S_{ab}+\sum_{L\ge1}F_{ab,L}(z),\qquad
F_{ab,L}(z)=\sum_{\gamma\in E^L}A_\gamma z_\gamma.
\tag{QC2}
$$

These are genuine analytic Taylor polynomials in the open polydisk. For an occupation vector `alpha=(alpha_e)_{e in E}`, `|alpha|=L`, let `Gamma_alpha` be the paths that visit each internal vertex `e` exactly `alpha_e` times and set `c_alpha=sum_{gamma in Gamma_alpha}A_gamma`. Orthogonality of independent phases gives

$$
\boxed{
q_L^{\rm qu}:=\mathbb E_Z|F_{ab,L}(Z)|^2
=\sum_{|\alpha|=L}|c_\alpha|^2.
}
\tag{QC3}
$$

The corresponding classical absorption-path weight is

$$
\boxed{
q_L^{\rm cl}:=\sum_{\gamma\in E^L}|A_\gamma|^2
=[P_{AE}P_{EE}^{L-1}P_{EA}]_{ab},\quad L\ge1.
}
\tag{QC4}
$$

Thus the **exact quantum-classical interference defect at length `L`** is

$$
\boxed{
q_L^{\rm qu}-q_L^{\rm cl}
=\sum_{|\alpha|=L}\ \sum_{\substack{\gamma,\gamma'\in\Gamma_\alpha\\\gamma\ne\gamma'}}
A_\gamma\overline{A_{\gamma'}}.
}
\tag{QC5}
$$

The right-hand side is real but need not be positive. Crucially, only pairs having the **same occupation multiset** survive phase integration; equal *time-ordering* of visits is not required. Summing the squares of individual paths alone computes the classical object (QC4), not the quantum one (QC3).

The identity (QC5) is first interpreted degree by degree, so no uncontrolled infinite interchange is involved. The full boundary quantities are obtained by `L²` convergence: contractivity of the matrix-valued Schur function on the polydisk yields its square-summable Hardy coefficients, and the original irreducibility assumption ensures the unitary boundary value almost surely. The classical path series converges by `rho(P_EE)<1`, as established in CE1. Consequently

$$
\mathbb E_Z T_{ab}-(K_A^{\rm cl})_{ab}
=\sum_{L\ge1}(q_L^{\rm qu}-q_L^{\rm cl}),
\tag{QC6}
$$

where the two individual series on the right are convergent sums of nonnegative numbers before subtraction. One must **not** replace (QC6) with the possibly divergent sum of the absolute values of every individual crossed path pair.

### QC2. Exact first nontrivial quantum correction and the cooperon norm

At `L=1`, each occupation vector labels a unique path, hence `q_1^qu=q_1^cl` exactly. At `L=2`, a nontrivial pair arises only from exchanging the ordered visits `(e,f)` and `(f,e)` for `e ne f`. Define

$$
x_e=S_{ae}\,\overline{S_{eb}},\qquad e\in E,
\quad B_{ef}=S_{ef}\,\overline{S_{fe}}.
\tag{QC7}
$$

Then the crossed term from an ordered pair `(e,f)` is exactly
`A_(e,f) conjugate(A_(f,e))=x_e B_ef conjugate(x_f)`. In vector notation, `B` is Hermitian, and

$$
\boxed{
q_2^{\rm qu}-q_2^{\rm cl}
=(\overline x)^*B_{EE}(\overline x)
-\sum_{e\in E}P_{ee}P_{ae}P_{eb}.
}
\tag{QC8}
$$

The diagonal term is subtracted because `(e,e)` is a single path, not a pair of different orders. From `sum_e P_ae<=1` and `max_e P_eb<=C_0/N`, one has

$$
\|x\|_2^2=\sum_e P_{ae}P_{eb}\le C_0/N.
\tag{QC9}
$$

Therefore the **original fixed cooperon gap** gives the rigorous estimate

$$
\boxed{\qquad
|q_2^{\rm qu}-q_2^{\rm cl}|
\le\left(\rho_*+\frac{C_0}{N}\right)\frac{C_0}{N}.
\qquad}
\tag{QC10}
$$

This is the exact lowest-degree place where the signed cooperon `B` enters the phase-averaged transmission. It does not control the sum of much longer crossed paths and is therefore not the main theorem.

### QC3. A universal factorial upper bound for every fixed internal degree

For each fixed occupation `alpha` of weight `L`, the number of ordered paths realizing it is the multinomial count `L!/prod_e alpha_e!`, at most `L!`. By Cauchy--Schwarz,

$$
|c_\alpha|^2\le L!\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_\alpha}|A_\gamma|^2.
$$

Summing and using the stochastic structure of `P`,

$$
\begin{aligned}
q_L^{\rm qu}
&\le L!\,q_L^{\rm cl}\\
&=L!\,[P_{AE}P_{EE}^{L-1}P_{EA}]_{ab}\\
&\le L!\,\frac{C_0}{N},\qquad L\ge1.
\end{aligned}
\tag{QC11}
$$

The final inequality follows because the last transition into the *specific* port `b` has probability at most `C_0/N`, and the preceding substochastic walk has total mass at most one. Together with `|S_ab|²<=C_0/N`, this proves

$$
\boxed{\quad
\mathbb E_Z\left|\sum_{L=0}^{L_-}F_{ab,L}(Z)\right|^2
\le\frac{C_0}{N}\sum_{L=0}^{L_-}L!,\qquad F_{ab,0}=S_{ab}.
\quad}
\tag{QC12}
$$

Orthogonality between distinct total degrees is exact because a common rotation of every internal phase sends `F_ab,L -> t^L F_ab,L`.

This bound does *not* require either spectral gap: it uses only unitarity, phase independence, and flatness. In particular, for every fixed `0<delta<1` and

$$
L_-(N)=\left\lfloor(1-\delta)\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}\right\rfloor,
\tag{QC13}
$$

Stirling's formula gives `sum_{L<=L_-}L! = N^{1-delta+o(1)}` and hence

$$
\boxed{\quad
\mathbb E_Z\left|\sum_{L=0}^{L_-}F_{ab,L}(Z)\right|^2
\le N^{-\delta+o(1)}.
\quad}
\tag{QC14}
$$

Thus **all coherent internal paths shorter than the specified logarithmic threshold carry asymptotically zero transmission energy**, uniformly over external pairs. This is not an assertion that individual longer paths behave classically.

## QC4. Combining the sharp quantum Kac identity with the short-path bound

For every fixed relative internal phase vector `eta`, the previously proved unitary-colligation identity (AP30) gives

$$
\sum_{L\ge1}L\sum_{a,b\in A}|F_{ab,L}(\eta)|^2=N-m.
\tag{QC15}
$$

For `m=2`, integrating in the relative phases yields, for **each** ordered pair `a != b`,

$$
\sum_{L>L_+}q_L^{\rm qu}\le\frac{N-2}{L_+}.
\tag{QC16}
$$

Take, for example, `L_+(N)=ceil(N log N)`. Then the tail is at most `1/log N`. Combined with (QC14), the phase-averaged two-channel *amplitude* has asymptotically all of its `L²` mass in a rigorously delimited window:

$$
\boxed{\quad
\mathbb E_Z\left|F_{ab}(Z)-\sum_{L_-<L\le L_+}F_{ab,L}(Z)\right|^2
\le N^{-\delta+o(1)}+\frac1{\log N}.
\quad}
\tag{QC17}
$$

The cross term vanishes exactly by orthogonality of different total degrees. Since the boundary matrix is unitary, `|F_ab|<=1`, and the truncated amplitude has `L²` norm at most one, the same estimate implies

$$
\mathbb E_Z\left|
|F_{ab}(Z)|^2-
\left|\sum_{L_-<L\le L_+}F_{ab,L}(Z)\right|^2
\right|
\le2\sqrt{N^{-\delta+o(1)}+(\log N)^{-1}}.
\tag{QC18}
$$

In words, short paths are too weak, and ultra-long paths are eliminated by the exact delay/degree identity. The *uncontrolled* interference is located between roughly `log N/loglog N` and `N log N` internal visits. This is a rigorous localization of the missing estimate, **not** a reduction of that estimate to a solved theorem.

## QC5. The exact remaining obstruction, checked against finite-dimensional examples

CE1 shows that

$$
\mathbb E_A\,(K_A^{\rm cl})_{ab}=\frac12+O_{C_0}(N^{-1})
\tag{QC19}
$$

for any uniformly chosen pair (indeed for each pair). Therefore

$$
\boxed{\quad
\mathbb E_{A,Z}T_N-\frac12
=\mathbb E_A\sum_{L\ge1}(q_L^{\rm qu}-q_L^{\rm cl})
+O_{C_0}(N^{-1}).
\quad}
\tag{QC20}
$$

The first crossed term `L=2` is `O(C_0 rho_*/N)`, and by (QC11) **all fixed degrees** are `O_{L,C_0}(N^{-1})`. However the complete signed series at degrees growing with `N` is still uncontrolled. The assumptions on `P` and `B` have not been shown to make its sum tend to zero. Even if this were established, it would prove only `E T_N->1/2`; the other moments needed for `Uniform[0,1]` would remain.

As an explicit warning: for the five-channel Fourier matrix, a numerical phase integral at the fixed retained pair `(0,1)` gives quantum `E T approximately 0.451` while the classical Markov Schur complement is exactly `0.5`. Thus quantum expectation is **not** equal to the classical hitting probability in general. This example has `||B||=1` and is *not* an asymptotic counterexample satisfying the original hypotheses. For the cubic-circulant admissible test families, classical `t_A` at `N=23,41,101,503` is respectively approximately `0.5092,0.5055,0.5051,0.5015`; these data check CE1, not the quantum conjecture.

**Independent checks.** Scripts `tc_new_check.py`, `tc_sherman_check.py` and `tc_cl_markov_check.py` numerically verify the path-occupation expansion through `L=4`, the exact equality (QC8) for random complex unitaries and a real cubic circulant (errors `<=2e-17`), Sherman--Morrison (CE9) for `N=7,13,101` (relative errors at the floating-point level), double stochasticity of the classical output, and the predicted approach of classical two-channel probabilities to `1/2`. The checks are diagnostics for the algebra, not a proof of the desired universal quantum law.

**Research conclusion of this appendix.** Classical mixing under the original assumptions is sufficient for deterministic multichannel absorption equipartition at rate `O(1/N)` entrywise. It also yields a sharp `O(N)` classical mean escape time. Coherent quantum amplitudes agree with the classical path sum at first internal degree, acquire the first explicit `B`-controlled interference correction at degree two, and necessarily derive their nontrivial total transmission from paths much longer than `log N/loglog N`. The exact quantum degree sum restricts relevant lengths to `O(N log N)`. Deriving universal interference cancellations at those mesoscopic and Heisenberg lengths remains open; no claim of the original theorem or an admissible counterexample is made.

## QC6. An exact finite-dimensional interference anomaly satisfying all three numerical inequalities

The asymptotic assumptions alone do not imply *finite-N* equality of the quantum mean with its classical counterpart. An explicit algebraic example with `P=J` and **strict** `||B||<1` makes the distinction unmistakable.

Let

$$
\boxed{
S_4=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}
1&1&1&1\\
1&i&-i&-1\\
1&-1&-1&1\\
1&-i&i&-1
\end{pmatrix}.
}
\tag{QC21}
$$

It is a column permutation of the four-dimensional Fourier matrix and hence unitary. Every entry has squared modulus `1/4`, so `P_4=J_4` and `C_0=1`. Its cooperon is

$$
B_4=\frac14\begin{pmatrix}
1&1&1&1\\
1&1&i&-i\\
1&-i&1&-i\\
1&i&i&1
\end{pmatrix},
\quad
\operatorname{spec}(B_4)
=\left\{\frac{2+\sqrt2}{4},\frac{2-\sqrt2}{4},
\frac{\sqrt2}{4},-\frac{\sqrt2}{4}\right\}.
\tag{QC22}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{\quad
\|P_4-J_4\|=0,\qquad
\|B_4\|=\frac{2+\sqrt2}{4}<1.
\quad}
\tag{QC23}
$$

For the ordered external pair `(a,b)=(0,1)` (indices `0,1,2,3`), close the internal channels `(2,3)` with independent uniform phases. The exact expectation can be calculated in two successive residue integrations. Eliminate channel `2` first with phase `z`, leaving a three-channel unitary matrix `Q(z)` on `(0,1,3)`. The elementary one-phase Schur identity for deletion of channel `3` gives

$$
\mathbb E_w T(z,w)
=|Q_{01}(z)|^2+
\frac{|Q_{03}(z)|^2|Q_{31}(z)|^2}{1-|Q_{33}(z)|^2}.
\tag{QC24}
$$

On the unit circle `|z|=1`, the expression simplifies **exactly** to

$$
g(z)=
\frac{-i\left[2z^4+(11+i)z^3+19z^2+(11-i)z+2\right]}
{(z+2)(2z+1)((1-i)z+1-2i)((2-i)z+1-i)}.
\tag{QC25}
$$

The contour integral `1/(2 pi i) int_{|z|=1} g(z) dz/z` has exactly three poles inside the disk, at

$$
0,\qquad -\frac12,\qquad \frac{-3+i}{5},
$$

with residues respectively

$$
\frac3{10}+\frac i{10},\qquad
\frac3{10}-\frac i{10},\qquad
-\frac1{15}.
$$

The residue theorem thus proves

$$
\boxed{\quad \mathbb E_ZT_{01}(S_4)=\frac8{15}\ne\frac12
=(K_{\{0,1\}}^{\rm cl})_{01}.\quad}
\tag{QC26}
$$

Repeating the same exact symbolic calculation for all twelve ordered pairs gives `8/15` for `(0,1),(1,0),(2,3),(3,2)` and `1/2` for the other eight. Consequently for a **uniformly chosen ordered pair**,

$$
\boxed{\quad
\mathbb E_{(I,J),Z}T_4=\frac{23}{45}\ne\frac12.
\quad}
\tag{QC27}
$$

These equalities are algebraic residue identities, not floating-point observations. A self-contained `sympy` verification is included immediately below. Since the main problem concerns the **limit as `N->infinity`**, (QC27) does **not** constitute the requested counterexample; it rules out an unjustified *exact* quantum-to-classical identification even when all three mixing bounds happen to hold in finite dimension.

### The tensor-product test — a genuine admissible infinite subsequence, not a counterexample

Take `H_m` to be the normalized symmetric Fourier matrix of size `m` and set

$$
\widehat S_{4m}=S_4\otimes H_m.
\tag{QC28}
$$

Then for **every** positive integer `m`, exactly

$$
\boxed{
\max_{ij}|(\widehat S_{4m})_{ij}|^2=\frac1{4m},\quad
P_{4m}=J_{4m},\quad
B_{4m}=B_4\otimes J_m,\quad
\|B_{4m}\|=\frac{2+\sqrt2}{4}<1.
}
\tag{QC29}
$$

Hence this is a particularly stringent and explicitly admissible test **along all sizes divisible by four**, with `C_0=1`, zero classical gap, and a cooperon norm bounded uniformly below one. The finite-size anomaly in (QC27) alone says nothing about this sequence's asymptotic transmission law. Direct random-phase simulations at `N=32,64,128,256` give means approximately `0.4582 +/- 0.0032`, `0.4698 +/- 0.0041`, `0.4850 +/- 0.0058`, `0.4898 +/- 0.0089`; these values are statistically compatible with eventual convergence to `1/2`, not with a proven non-uniform limit. The same test with Walsh--Hadamard factors behaves similarly. **No admissible asymptotic counterexample has been demonstrated.**

### Exact symbolic verification of (QC21)--(QC27)

```python
import sympy as s
z=s.symbols('z'); I=s.I
S=s.Matrix([[1,1,1,1],[1,I,-I,-1],[1,-1,-1,1],[1,-I,I,-1]])/2
B=s.Matrix([[s.simplify(S[i,j]*s.conjugate(S[j,i]))
             for j in range(4)] for i in range(4)])
assert B.eigenvals()=={s.Rational(1,2)-s.sqrt(2)/4:1,
 s.Rational(1,2)+s.sqrt(2)/4:1,-s.sqrt(2)/4:1,s.sqrt(2)/4:1}
assert s.simplify(S*S.conjugate().T-s.eye(4))==s.zeros(4)

def conjugate_on_circle(expr):
 return s.conjugate(expr).subs(s.conjugate(z),1/z)
def modulus_square(expr):
 return s.cancel(expr*conjugate_on_circle(expr))

answers=[]
for a in range(4):
 for b in range(4):
  if a==b: continue
  e,f=[j for j in range(4) if j not in (a,b)]
  A=[a,b,f]
  Q=s.Matrix([[s.cancel(S[i,j]+z/(1-z*S[e,e])*S[i,e]*S[e,j])
               for j in A] for i in A])
  g=s.factor(modulus_square(Q[0,1]) +
       modulus_square(Q[0,2])*modulus_square(Q[2,1]) /
       (1-modulus_square(Q[2,2])))
  poles=[p for p in s.solve(s.denom(g),z)
         if abs(complex(p.evalf()))<1-1e-7]
  value=s.simplify(sum(s.residue(g/z,z,p) for p in [0]+poles))
  answers.append(value)
print('exact ordered-pair means:', answers)
assert sorted(answers)==[s.Rational(1,2)]*8+[s.Rational(8,15)]*4
assert s.simplify(sum(answers)/12)==s.Rational(23,45)
print('uniform-pair mean:', s.simplify(sum(answers)/12))
```

**Consolidated outstanding requirement.** To settle the original conjecture one must control, for all fixed `k>=1`, the full unregularized long-path phase interference in `E T_N^k`, or equivalently prove the fractional-overlap criterion (FM5). CE1, QC10, QC14, QC17 and the finite anomaly (QC27) are rigorous results that expose the mechanism and its scale, but leave this essential step unproved.

## QC7. New all-moment time-window theorem (not merely the first moment)

The previous short- and long-degree estimates actually extend to **every** polynomial moment of the transmission, by a useful Hardy-space argument. This avoids the naive, and much worse, convolution estimates for the coefficients of `F_ab^k`.

Let `f(z)=F_ab(z)` be the analytic scalar two-port amplitude and write, for each integer `k>=1`,

$$
f(z)^k=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb N^E}c_\alpha^{(k)}z^\alpha,
\qquad q_L^{(k)}=\sum_{|\alpha|=L}|c_\alpha^{(k)}|^2.
\tag{QC30}
$$

Because `f` is bounded by one on the open polydisk and has unitary-matrix boundary values, multivariate Hardy Parseval gives

$$
\boxed{\quad \mathbb E_Z T_{ab}^k=\sum_{L\ge0}q_L^{(k)}.\quad}
\tag{QC31}
$$

Let `\Pi_{\le L}` denote orthogonal projection in the Hardy space of the phase torus onto total Fourier degree at most `L`. Analyticity, nonnegativity of Fourier degrees, and `|f|<=1` give the exact projection identity and norm estimate

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Pi_{\le L}(f^k)
&=\Pi_{\le L}\big[(\Pi_{\le L}f)f^{k-1}\big],\\
\|\Pi_{\le L}(f^k)\|_{L^2(\mathbb T^E)}
&\le\|(\Pi_{\le L}f)f^{k-1}\|_2
\le\|\Pi_{\le L}f\|_2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{QC32}
$$

Thus **uniformly for all integers `k>=1`**,

$$
\boxed{\quad
\sum_{l=0}^Lq_l^{(k)}\le\sum_{l=0}^Lq_l^{(1)}
\le\frac{C_0}{N}\sum_{l=0}^L l!.
\quad}
\tag{QC33}
$$

For the high-degree end, fix the relative internal phases `eta`, and put `g(t)=f(t eta)`. The unitary colligation makes `g` a scalar Schur function, `|g(t)|<=1` for `|t|<1`. The classical Dirichlet identity, with normalized area measure `dA/pi`, is

$$
\int_{|t|<1}|g'(t)|^2\frac{dA(t)}\pi
=\sum_{L\ge1}L|[t^L]g|^2.
\tag{QC34}
$$

Since `(g^k)'=k g^{k-1}g'`, its Dirichlet energy satisfies

$$
\sum_{L\ge1}L|[t^L]g^k|^2
\le k^2\sum_{L\ge1}L|[t^L]g|^2
\le k^2(N-2).
\tag{QC35}
$$

The last inequality follows from the **exact** matrix degree/quantum-Kac identity (AP30), by keeping just the entry `(a,b)`. Finally average over independent uniform relative phases, identifying squared coefficient norms at each total degree. This yields

$$
\boxed{\quad
\sum_{L>L_+}q_L^{(k)}\le\frac{k^2(N-2)}{L_+},
\qquad k\ge1.
\quad}
\tag{QC36}
$$

Combining (QC31), (QC33), and (QC36), we have an **explicit fully unregularized positive-series approximation to every moment**:

$$
\boxed{
0\le
\mathbb E_Z T_{ab}^k-
\sum_{L_-<L\le L_+}q_L^{(k)}
\le
\frac{C_0}{N}\sum_{l=0}^{L_-}l!
+\frac{k^2(N-2)}{L_+}.
}
\tag{QC37}
$$

Here `L_-` and `L_+` can be *any* integers with `0<=L_-<L_+`; the statement is deterministic in the input `S` and uniform in the external pair. For the scales `L_- = floor((1-delta) log N/loglog N)` and `L_+=ceil(N log N)`, for each fixed `k`,

$$
\boxed{
\mathbb E_Z T_{ab}^k
=\sum_{L_-<L\le L_+}q_L^{(k)}
+O_{C_0,k,\delta}\big(N^{-\delta+o(1)}+(\log N)^{-1}\big).
}
\tag{QC38}
$$

In fact the low-end bound is **independent of `k`**, and the upper-end estimate works simultaneously for any `k=o(\sqrt{\log N})`. This theorem encompasses the entire moment formulation of the requested limit, not just the first moment, but still does **not** evaluate the positive window mass. The essential remaining estimate is to show that this mass converges to `1/(k+1)` for each fixed `k`. No such evaluation or admissible asymptotic counterexample has been obtained.
